    209. Which collections does GridFS use and how large are the chunks?

    GridFS stores metadata in fs.files and binary chunks in fs.chunks, with an index on the files id and chunk number. The default chunk size is 255 KB, and it can be configured per bucket. Reads reassemble chunks in order, so range requests still fetch whole chunks.

    210. What is a MongoDB view and how do you create one?

    A view is a read-only, named query defined by an aggregation pipeline, stored as metadata in the database. Creating one uses db.createCollection(name, { viewOn: ..., pipeline: [...] }) or db.createView(). Clients query it like a collection, which lets you expose a filtered or reshaped projection without duplicating data.

    211. Are views materialized, and how do you create an on-demand materialized view?

    Standard views are computed on every read and consume no storage, so they are only as fast as their underlying pipeline. To materialize results, end a pipeline with $out or $merge into a real collection and query that collection instead. The trade-off is that the data is only as fresh as the last refresh, which you schedule yourself.

    212. What is collation and how do you run a case-insensitive query?

    Collation defines language-aware comparison rules for strings, including case and accent handling. To make a query case-insensitive, specify a collation such as { locale: "en", strength: 2 } on the query, and create the index with the same collation so it can be used. The collation must match between index and query or the index is ignored.

    db.users.find({ email: "ANA@EXAMPLE.COM" }).collation({ locale: "en", strength: 2 });

    213. What does collation strength control?

    Strength sets how many differences are considered significant: 1 ignores case and accents, 2 ignores case but considers accents, and 3, the default, is case and accent sensitive. Higher strengths also consider case variants and, at 4 or 5, punctuation or code point differences. Choose the lowest strength that satisfies the requirement, since it affects index usability.

    214. What is read concern and which levels are commonly used?

    Read concern controls the durability and isolation guarantees of data returned by a read. "local" is the default and may return data that could still be rolled back, "majority" returns data acknowledged by most replica set members, and "snapshot" provides a consistent point-in-time view, mainly for transactions. "linearizable" exists for reads that must see all prior majority-acknowledged writes.

    215. What does write concern specify?

    Write concern states how many members must acknowledge a write before the driver reports success, using w, plus j for journaling and wtimeout for a bound. w: 1 acknowledges from the primary only and is fast, while w: "majority" survives a failover. Stronger write concern costs latency but reduces the chance of rolled-back writes.

    db.orders.insertOne(doc, { writeConcern: { w: "majority", j: true } })

    216. What is a replica set and which roles do its members play?

    A replica set is a group of mongod instances that maintain the same data set for redundancy and high availability. Exactly one member is primary and accepts writes, while secondaries replicate the oplog and can serve reads if configured. Elections promote a secondary automatically when the primary becomes unavailable.

    217. What is read preference and which modes exist?

    Read preference decides which replica set members a client sends reads to. Modes include primary, primaryPreferred, secondary, secondaryPreferred and nearest. Secondary reads may return slightly stale data, so use primary for read-after-write guarantees.

    218. What are retryable writes?

    Retryable writes let the driver automatically retry certain single-document write operations once if a transient network error or failover occurs. MongoDB tracks a session id and transaction number per write so a retried write is applied at most once. They require a replica set or sharded cluster and a supporting write concern, and multi-document operations need a transaction instead.

    219. What is a session and what does causal consistency mean?

    A session groups related operations so they share causal ordering metadata, which drivers manage automatically. With causal consistency enabled, a read in the same session is guaranteed to see writes performed earlier in that session, even when reading from a secondary. This is achieved by tracking the operation time and cluster time on the session.

    220. What are change streams?

    Change streams let an application subscribe to a real-time stream of data changes on a collection, database or deployment. They emit resume-token-bearing events such as insert, update, replace, delete and invalidate, without tailing the oplog manually. Because they use the oplog, they require a replica set or sharded cluster and majority read concern.

    const cs = db.orders.watch([{ $match: { "operationType": "insert" } }]);
while (!cs.isClosed()) { const next = await cs.next(); printjson(next.fullDocument); }

    221. How do you resume a change stream after a disconnect?

    Each event includes a _id resume token, which you persist after processing and pass back through resumeAfter when reopening the stream. For an event that was not fully processed, startAfter is the correct option. The oplog window limits how far back a resume can succeed, so long outages can make a token unusable.

    222. What is $jsonSchema validation used for?

    $jsonSchema lets you attach a JSON Schema validator to a collection so inserts and updates must match declared field types and required properties. You can add it at creation with validator or later with collMod. Validation happens on write, which protects data quality close to the database rather than only in the application.

    db.createCollection("users", { validator: { $jsonSchema: {
  bsonType: "object", required: ["email"],
  properties: { email: { bsonType: "string" } } } } });

    223. What do validationLevel and validationAction control?

    validationLevel decides which documents are checked: strict validates all inserts and updates, moderate only validates documents that are already valid, and off disables validation. validationAction decides whether a violation is rejected with error or merely logged with warn. Starting with warn is a safe way to roll out a new schema.

    224. How do you run a multi-document transaction in MongoDB?

    Start a session, call startTransaction(), run the operations with that session, then commitTransaction() and end the session. Any error should be followed by abortTransaction(). Transactions require a replica set or sharded cluster and explicit session passing on every operation.

    const s = db.getMongo().startSession();
s.startTransaction();
s.getDatabase("shop").orders.insertOne({ _id: 1, total: 10 });
s.commitTransaction();

    225. What are the main limitations of transactions?

    A transaction has a default lifetime limit of 60 seconds, controlled by transactionLifetimeLimitSeconds, and each operation must fit within the 16 MB oplog entry limit. Transactions block on conflicting writes, so long-running ones increase lock contention. They are also not a substitute for a good schema; cross-document atomicity is expensive compared with single-document atomic writes.

    226. What is the database profiler and which levels does it support?

    The profiler records slow or all operations in the system.profile capped collection for later analysis. Level 0 is off, level 1 records operations slower than slowms, and level 2 records every operation. Level 2 is heavy, so enable it briefly in production and inspect the results with queries on system.profile.

    db.setProfilingLevel(1, { slowms: 100 });
db.system.profile.find().sort({ ts: -1 }).limit(5);

    227. What is db.currentOp used for?

    db.currentOp() shows operations currently running on the server, including their namespace, duration, plan summary and waiting state. It is the first tool for diagnosing a slow query or a stuck operation in progress. Filter by secs_running or namespace, and use db.killOp() only for operations you are certain are safe to terminate.

    db.currentOp({ secs_running: { $gt: 5 }, active: true });

    228. What is the difference between mongostat and mongotop?

    mongostat prints a per-second snapshot of server counters such as inserts, queries, updates, deletes, connections and page faults. mongotop reports how much time each collection spends on reads and writes, which points to hot collections. Together they separate overall load from per-collection hotspots.

    229. Which Atlas UI capabilities matter most for day-to-day operations?

    The cluster view shows topology, region, tier and connection string, and supports scaling and pausing the cluster. The backup section manages snapshots and point-in-time restores, while alerts can notify on replication lag, slow queries and disk usage. The built-in Performance Advisor and Query Profiler surface slow operations and index suggestions without shell access.

    230. How would you model a one-to-few relationship?

    For a small, bounded number of related items, embed them as an array of subdocuments inside the parent, for example a few addresses on a user. This keeps related data in one document, so a single read returns everything and updates stay atomic. The approach works while the array stays small and is not updated concurrently by many writers.

    { _id: 1, name: "Ana", addresses: [ { city: "Bogota" }, { city: "Medellin" } ] }

    231. How would you model a one-to-many relationship?

    When the number of children can grow into the hundreds, store the parent id on each child document and index that field, rather than keeping an unbounded array on the parent. Application code queries children by parent id, optionally with pagination. This keeps documents small and avoids rewriting a growing array on every insert.

    db.orders.createIndex({ customerId: 1 });
db.orders.find({ customerId: 42 }).sort({ createdAt: -1 }).limit(20);

    232. How would you model a one-to-squillions relationship?

    For very high cardinality, such as log lines or sensor readings per device, never embed the children; put the parent reference on the child and index it. The parent document then stays small regardless of volume. Time series collections or bucketing are often a better fit than one document per child for this shape.

    233. How do you decide between embedding and referencing?

    Embed when the data is read together, the child count is bounded, and the child has little independent life. Reference when the data is large or unbounded, updated independently, accessed separately, or shared by many parents. Always check the 16 MB document limit and the write pattern, since embedding makes every write rewrite the whole document.

    234. What is the bucket pattern and what problem does it solve?

    The bucket pattern groups many small events into one document, typically by time window, storing an array of measurements plus precomputed counts and totals. It dramatically reduces the number of documents and index entries for high-volume time-ordered data. Reads then fetch one bucket instead of hundreds of documents.

    { sensor: "s1", bucketStart: ISODate("2026-01-01T00:00:00Z"),
  count: 60, total: 1432, samples: [ { t: 1, v: 24 }, { t: 2, v: 25 } ] }

    235. What is the computed pattern?

    The computed pattern stores a derived value, such as a total, average or count, directly in the document so reads avoid recomputing it. It pays off when reads greatly outnumber writes and the computation is expensive, for example a movie rating average. The trade-off is that the application must update the computed field whenever the underlying data changes.

    236. When does an extended reference go stale, and how do you keep it current?

    An extended reference is stale from the moment the source document changes: the copy inside the child keeps the old value until something updates it. The cheap fields to copy are the ones that never change after creation, such as a customer's signup country or a product's SKU prefix. The expensive ones are any field the business can edit, like a name or a price.

    Keeping it current needs an explicit writer: either the update path that touches the source also fans out to the copies, or a change stream listener patches them asynchronously. Either way, accept that reads can see a copy a few milliseconds behind, and stamp the embedded block with updatedAt so a background job can find and repair the drift.

    237. Why add a schema version field to documents?

    A schemaVersion field records which shape a document follows, so application code can handle several versions during a rolling migration. It makes migrations explicit and lets you find documents that still need upgrading. Without it, distinguishing old and new shapes relies on guessing from missing or present fields.

    { _id: 1, schemaVersion: 2, name: "Ana", fullName: { first: "Ana", last: "Diaz" } }

    238. How do you handle a schema migration in MongoDB?

    Either migrate lazily, converting documents as they are read or written, or backfill in batches with a script. Lazy migration spreads the work but requires dual-read code paths; batch migration gives a clean end state but must be throttled. Whichever you choose, deploy application code that understands both shapes before and after the change.

    239. How do you migrate a large collection without downtime?

    Process documents in small batches filtered by an indexed field such as _id, update each batch with bulkWrite, and pause between batches to limit load. Track the last processed key so the job can resume after a failure. Run it during low traffic and monitor replication lag and slow queries while it runs.

    db.users.find({ _id: { $gt: lastId }, schemaVersion: { $exists: false } })
  .sort({ _id: 1 }).limit(1000);

    240. What is the 16 MB document size limit?

    A single BSON document cannot exceed 16 MB, which is a hard limit enforced by the server on insert and update. The limit applies to the encoded BSON, including field names and array elements. Exceeding it fails the write, and no index or option can raise the limit.

    241. What do you do when data no longer fits in one document?

    Split the data using the bucket or subset pattern, move large binaries into GridFS, or promote the embedded array into its own collection with a parent reference. Each option trades a single read for smaller documents and more queries. The important step is recognizing the growth trend before writes start failing.

    242. Which compression options does WiredTiger support?

    WiredTiger supports snappy, which is the default and favours speed, zlib for better compression at higher CPU cost, and zstd as a balanced option available in current versions. Indexes use prefix compression by default and can also be compressed. The choice affects CPU versus disk usage, not query semantics.

    243. How do you enable or change collection compression?

    Pass a WiredTiger storage engine configuration when creating the collection, for example a blockCompressor of zstd. Changing compression on an existing collection requires rewriting its data, typically with an initial sync or a rebuild such as compact or a resync. Verify the effect by comparing storage size and CPU after the change.

    db.createCollection("events", { storageEngine: { wiredTiger: {
  configString: "block_compressor=zstd" } } });

    244. How does a multikey index work?

    When you index a field that holds an array, MongoDB creates one index entry per array element, and the index is marked multikey. A query on an element value can then use the index without scanning the array. Multikey indexes are larger than scalar indexes and have restrictions on how bounds can be combined.

    db.articles.createIndex({ tags: 1 });
db.articles.find({ tags: "mongodb" });

    245. Why can you not create a compound index on two array fields?

    Indexing two array fields in the same compound index would produce a cartesian product of index entries for every document, so MongoDB rejects it with a "cannot index parallel arrays" error. The workaround is to index one array field and filter the other in the query, or to restructure the data. A compound index may include at most one array field.

    246. Why is $elemMatch important when querying arrays?

    Without $elemMatch, separate conditions on array elements can be satisfied by different elements of the same document. $elemMatch requires a single element to match all conditions, which is usually the intended semantics. It also helps the planner use a multikey index more precisely.

    db.orders.find({ items: { $elemMatch: { sku: "A1", qty: { $gte: 2 } } } })

    247. Why is skip and limit pagination slow on large collections?

    skip(n) makes the server walk and discard the first n documents, so the cost grows with the page number even when an index is used. Deep pages therefore become progressively slower and put load on the server. It is acceptable for small offsets or admin screens, but not for infinite scrolling.

    248. What is keyset pagination and how does it work?

    Keyset pagination remembers the last seen sort value and queries for documents after it, for example { _id: { $gt: lastId } } sorted ascending. Each page costs roughly the same because the index seeks directly to the starting point. Include a unique tiebreaker field so pages cannot skip or repeat documents.

    db.orders.find({ createdAt: { $lt: lastDate } }).sort({ createdAt: -1 }).limit(20);

    249. What is the ESR rule for compound indexes?

    ESR stands for Equality, Sort, Range: put fields used in equality filters first, then fields used for sorting, then range-filtered fields. This ordering lets the index satisfy the filter and the sort while scanning a contiguous range. Following ESR usually removes the need for an in-memory sort.

    db.orders.createIndex({ status: 1, createdAt: -1, total: 1 });

    250. How can an index satisfy a sort?

    An index can return documents already ordered if the sort fields form a prefix of the index key pattern and the directions match, either exactly or fully reversed. Equality-constrained leading fields do not break the ordering of the remaining fields. If the sort direction or prefix does not match, the server must perform a blocking sort.

    251. When can a sort not use an index, and what happens then?

    A sort cannot use an index when the requested order does not match an index prefix and direction, when the sort is on a computed or unwound field, or when the pipeline has already reshaped documents. The server then performs an in-memory sort, limited to 100 MB per stage unless disk use is allowed. Large blocking sorts are a frequent cause of slow queries and memory errors.

    252. When does a covered query stop being covered?

    It stops the moment the planner needs anything the index does not contain. Adding a field to the projection that is not in the index is the usual cause, and so is leaving _id in the results when the index was not built with it. A filter on a field outside the index has the same effect, because the server must fetch the document to test it.

    Other quiet breakers: a multikey index where the query has to inspect individual array elements, an $or whose branches use different indexes, and a collation the index was not created with. Check with explain(); if a FETCH stage appears where there was none before, coverage was lost.

    253. How do you confirm that a query is covered?

    Run explain("executionStats") and check that the plan has no FETCH stage and that totalDocsExamined is 0. A plan showing only IXSCAN with PROJECTION_COVERED confirms the index answered everything. If totalDocsExamined is greater than zero, the query is not covered.

    254. How does an $or query use indexes?

    Each branch of an $or is planned independently and can use its own index, with the results merged and de-duplicated. If any branch cannot use an index, the whole query degrades to a collection scan. That is why an unindexed clause inside $or can make an otherwise fast query slow.

    db.users.find({ $or: [ { email: "a@b.com" }, { phone: "+57300" } ] });

    255. When should you use $in instead of $or?

    When all values apply to the same field, $in is simpler and lets the planner build a single index scan with multiple bounds. $or is needed when the conditions apply to different fields or contain separate operators. $in also reads more clearly and is easier for the optimizer to handle.

    db.orders.find({ status: { $in: ["paid", "shipped"] } });

    256. Why are $ne and $nin often slow?

    Negation operators must examine every candidate value to prove that it is not excluded, so they cannot use tight index bounds. With no other selective condition, the server ends up scanning the collection or the whole index. Rewriting the query around positive, selective predicates is usually the fix.

    257. Does $or inside an aggregation $match use indexes?

    Yes, a leading $match with $or is planned like the same predicate in find(), so each branch can use an index. The same rule applies: an unindexed branch forces a collection scan. Placing the $or after a stage that reshapes documents removes any chance of index use.

    258. What is the difference between $count in aggregation and countDocuments()?

    countDocuments() runs an aggregation with a $match and $group internally, so it returns an accurate count for a filter but scans matching index entries. The $count stage does the same work inside a pipeline. Neither can rely on collection metadata, which is why both can be slow on very large collections with broad filters.

    259. When is estimatedDocumentCount() appropriate?

    estimatedDocumentCount() reads collection metadata instead of scanning documents, so it is fast but cannot take a filter. The value can be slightly inaccurate after an unclean shutdown. Use it for dashboards and approximate totals, and use countDocuments() when the number must be exact.

    260. How do you return a page of results together with the total count in one round trip?

    Use $facet with one sub-pipeline for the page using $sort, $skip and $limit, and another for the total using $count. Both run over the same filtered input, which saves a second query and guarantees a consistent snapshot. The facet buffers the input, so keep the pre-facet $match selective.

    { $facet: {
  data: [ { $sort: { createdAt: -1 } }, { $skip: 20 }, { $limit: 20 } ],
  meta: [ { $count: "total" } ] } }

    261. How do you produce a value distribution report with counts?

    Group by the field and count documents per value, then sort descending by the count; $sortByCount does exactly this in one stage. This gives a quick top-N view of categories, browsers or statuses. Add a $limit afterwards to keep only the most common values.

    db.events.aggregate([ { $sortByCount: "$browser" }, { $limit: 10 } ]);

    262. How do you flatten an embedded subdocument into the root?

    Use $replaceWith with $mergeObjects combining the subdocument and $$ROOT, so the subdocument fields become top-level fields. The nested copy can then be removed with $unset. This is common before exporting data or feeding a downstream system that expects a flat shape.

    { $replaceWith: { $mergeObjects: ["$address", "$$ROOT"] } },
{ $unset: "address" }

    263. How do you group numeric values into labelled ranges in a pipeline?

    Use $switch or nested $cond in a $addFields stage to derive a label, then group by that label. This keeps the range logic explicit and readable compared with deeply nested conditions. For evenly spaced ranges, $bucket is a better fit.

    { $addFields: { band: { $switch: { branches: [
  { case: { $lt: ["$total", 50] }, then: "low" },
  { case: { $lt: ["$total", 200] }, then: "mid" } ], default: "high" } } } }

    264. What is $$NOW and when is it useful?

    $$NOW returns the current date and time as a single consistent value for the whole pipeline execution. It is useful for computing ages, retention windows or report periods without sending the timestamp from the client. Because it is evaluated once per operation, all documents are compared against the same instant.

    { $project: { ageDays: { $dateDiff: { startDate: "$createdAt", endDate: "$$NOW", unit: "day" } } } }

    265. What does $$REMOVE do?

    $$REMOVE evaluates to a missing value, so assigning it in $addFields or $project removes the field from the output document. It is ideal for conditional exclusion, where a field should disappear only for some documents. It cannot be used to remove fields in a find() projection.

    { $addFields: { internalNote: { $cond: ["$isAdmin", "$note", "$$REMOVE"] } } }

    266. How do you get the latest document per group?

    Sort by the timestamp descending before grouping, then take { $first: "$$ROOT" } in the group. The sort must happen before the group, since $first only reflects the incoming order. Sorting ascending and using $last is equivalent but usually less intuitive.

    [ { $sort: { createdAt: -1 } },
  { $group: { _id: "$customerId", latest: { $first: "$$ROOT" } } } ]

    267. How do you count distinct values per group?

    Collect values with $addToSet and then take $size of the resulting array in a following $project or $addFields. This is a common analytics question because no distinct accumulator exists. The array is held in memory, so for very high cardinality the group can hit the memory limit.

    { $group: { _id: "$country", cities: { $addToSet: "$city" } } },
{ $project: { distinctCities: { $size: "$cities" } } }

    268. How do you rebuild an array after unwinding it?

    After $unwind, group by the parent document id and use $push to collect the elements again, ideally after sorting them into the desired order. This is the standard technique for filtering an array and keeping the result as an array. Remember that $push preserves the incoming order, so sort before grouping if order matters.

    [ { $unwind: "$items" }, { $match: { "items.qty": { $gt: 0 } } },
  { $group: { _id: "$_id", items: { $push: "$items" } } } ]

    269. How do you avoid running $lookup on every read?

    Denormalize the few fields needed at read time using the extended reference or computed pattern, so the common query becomes a single-document read. Keep $lookup for admin views, reports and rare paths where freshness matters more than latency. The cost of denormalization is that changes must be propagated to the copies.

    270. How do you approach reading MongoDB log lines when debugging?

    MongoDB writes structured JSON log entries containing a timestamp, severity, component, message id and attributes. Slow operations appear with fields such as planSummary, docsExamined, keysExamined and durationMillis. Filtering on the message id for slow queries and on planSummary: "COLLSCAN" quickly reveals missing indexes.

    Select-String -Path mongod.log -Pattern '"planSummary":"COLLSCAN"'

    271. How do you find missing indexes from slow query output?

    Look for operations with a high ratio of documents examined to documents returned, and for COLLSCAN in the plan summary. Then inspect the filter and sort fields and design a compound index following the ESR rule. Confirm the improvement with explain("executionStats") rather than assuming.

    272. Can you force an index in an aggregation pipeline?

    Yes, aggregate() accepts a hint option naming an index, and the pipeline's leading $match and $sort can then use it. Hints are useful for testing or pinning a stable plan, but they become brittle when data distribution changes. Prefer fixing the index design over hard-coding a hint in production.

    db.orders.aggregate(pipeline, { hint: { status: 1, createdAt: -1 } });

    273. Why should a query return only the fields it needs?

    Projecting only required fields reduces network payload and memory use on both server and client. It also enables covered queries when the projection matches an index. Returning whole documents by default is convenient in development but costly on wide documents with large arrays.

    274. How do you handle time zones in date aggregation?

    Date operators accept a timezone option using an Olson identifier such as America/Bogota, which shifts the calendar calculations to that zone. Without it, grouping by day happens in UTC and produces off-by-hours buckets for local reports. Store dates in UTC and apply the timezone only when grouping or formatting.

    275. How do you match documents whose array contains all specified values?

    Use $all with an array of values; it matches only documents containing every listed element. A plain array in the query is treated as an exact match of the whole array, which is usually not what you want. $all can use a multikey index for the elements.

    db.articles.find({ tags: { $all: ["mongodb", "indexes"] } });

    276. How do you update a specific element inside an array?

    Use $set with the positional $ operator, or arrayFilters with $[elem] when the element is identified by a condition rather than a position. arrayFilters is the reliable choice when several elements could match. Each update rewrites the whole document, so very large arrays are expensive to modify.

    db.orders.updateOne({ _id: 1 },
  { $set: { "items.$[i].qty": 5 } },
  { arrayFilters: [ { "i.sku": "A1" } ] });

    277. How do you return only the first matching array element in a find query?

    Use a projection with $elemMatch on the array field, which returns just the first element matching the condition. A plain field projection would return the entire array. This keeps responses small for documents with large arrays.

    db.orders.find({ _id: 1 }, { items: { $elemMatch: { sku: "A1" } } });

    278. How should monetary values be stored in MongoDB?

    Store them as Decimal128 when exact decimal arithmetic is required, or as integer minor units when the application can handle scaling. Binary floating point can introduce rounding errors that are unacceptable for money. Aggregation arithmetic on Decimal128 preserves decimal precision.

    { price: NumberDecimal("19.99"), currency: "COP" }

    279. How do you make a read-modify-write operation atomic?

    Use a single-document atomic operator such as findOneAndUpdate with $inc, $set or an update pipeline, instead of reading, modifying in the application and writing back. MongoDB guarantees atomicity per document, so concurrent updates cannot interleave inside that operation. Multi-document atomicity requires a transaction.

    db.counters.findOneAndUpdate({ _id: "orders" }, { $inc: { seq: 1 } },
  { returnDocument: "after", upsert: true });

    280. How do you keep only the most recent N items in an array?

    Use $push with $each and $slice in an update so the array is trimmed to the newest N elements after insertion. The negative slice count keeps elements from the end of the array. This is a practical way to store a bounded recent-history list without a separate collection.

    db.users.updateOne({ _id: 1 },
  { $push: { recentViews: { $each: [ { sku: "A1" } ], $slice: -10 } } });

    281. How do you create a free Atlas cluster, and what does the free tier include?

    In the Atlas UI choose Database → Build a Database, pick the M0 (free) tier, select a cloud provider and a region close to your users, name the cluster and deploy. Provisioning takes a few minutes and Atlas walks you through a security quickstart where you create the first database user and add your IP address.

    An M0 cluster gives you about 512 MB of storage on shared infrastructure: no dedicated RAM or IOPS, so throughput is modest and noisy neighbours are possible. It is limited to one free cluster per project, has no automated cloud backups, and some features (dedicated tiers, certain backup and scaling options) require a paid tier such as M10 or higher. It is perfect for learning, demos and small prototypes, not for production.

    282. What is the Atlas IP access list, and why does it block my connection?

    The IP access list (formerly called the whitelist) is a set of IP addresses or CIDR blocks that Atlas accepts TCP connections from. It is enforced by the cluster's front end before authentication, so a correct username and password still fail if your address is not listed: you typically see a timeout or an error such as connection ... refused or not allowed to connect.

    Add your current public IP under Network Access → Add IP Address. Cloud runtimes, CI agents and home connections change addresses often, which is the usual reason a working app suddenly cannot connect. You can add 0.0.0.0/0 to allow everything, but that exposes the cluster to the internet and should be limited to throwaway sandboxes.

    283. How do you create a database user in Atlas, and which role should you give it?

    Atlas database users are separate from your Atlas account: you create them under Database Access → Add New Database User, choosing password (SCRAM) or certificate authentication. Then you assign roles scoped to a database.

    Give the application the smallest role that works: readWrite on the single application database covers normal CRUD, while read fits reporting or analytics services. Cluster-wide roles such as readAnyDatabase, dbAdminAnyDatabase or atlasAdmin should be reserved for operational tooling and humans, never for an app server, because a compromised process would reach every database in the cluster.

    mongosh "mongodb+srv://cluster0.abcde.mongodb.net/" \
  --username appUser --authenticationDatabase admin

    284. How do you connect Compass or mongosh to an Atlas cluster with the mongodb+srv string?

    Open the cluster in Atlas, click Connect, and pick the tool: Compass gives you a paste-ready string, Shell gives the mongosh command with your cluster host. The string looks like this:

    mongodb+srv://appUser:Secret123@cluster0.abcde.mongodb.net/shop?retryWrites=true&w=majority

    Two practical details cause most failures. First, special characters in the password must be percent-encoded (@ becomes %40, : becomes %3A). Second, the path segment (/shop) is the default database, while authentication normally goes against admin. The +srv form resolves the replica set members from DNS, and TLS is enabled for you, so no extra options are needed.

    285. Where do you find metrics and backups in the Atlas UI?

    Select the cluster and open the Metrics tab: it charts operations per second, connections, network throughput, disk utilisation, query targeting and WiredTiger cache pressure, with a configurable time range. The Real-Time Performance panel shows currently running operations and slow queries, which is the fastest way to see what is happening right now.

    Backups live in the Backup tab, where you can see snapshots, configure a backup policy and restore to a point in time or to a new cluster. Free and shared tiers do not include automated cloud backups, so on M0 you must export your own data with mongodump. Alerts can be wired to email or Slack from the project settings.

    286. How do you list the indexes on a collection, and how do you read the output?

    Use getIndexes(), which returns one document per index with the key pattern, the generated name and the options.

    db.users.getIndexes()

[
  { v: 2, key: { _id: 1 }, name: "_id_" },
  { v: 2, key: { email: 1 }, name: "email_1", unique: true },
  { v: 2, key: { country: 1, createdAt: -1 }, name: "country_1_createdAt_-1" }
]

    Read it left to right: key is the indexed field order (1 ascending, -1 descending, "text" or "2dsphere" for special types), name is what you pass to dropIndex(), and options such as unique, sparse, partialFilterExpression or expireAfterSeconds describe behaviour. v: 2 is the current index format version.

    287. When should you drop an index, and how do you do it?

    Drop an index when it duplicates the prefix of a longer compound index, when it is never used, or when a write-heavy collection pays more in maintenance than the index returns in reads. Check usage first with db.users.aggregate([{ $indexStats: {} }]): an accesses.ops counter that stays near zero after a full workload cycle is strong evidence.

    db.users.dropIndex("email_1")
db.users.dropIndex({ country: 1, createdAt: -1 })

    Do it in staging first, and remember that dropping frees disk and write overhead immediately but cannot be undone except by recreating it, which on a large collection is an expensive build. The _id index cannot be dropped.

    288. How do you rebuild an index in MongoDB, and when is it worth doing?

    MongoDB has no REBUILD INDEX statement like some relational engines. The reliable approach is to drop the index and create it again with the options you want; on a replica set the build is replicated to secondaries, and from 4.4 you can create an index as hidden: true first, then unhide it once it is built, so it never becomes visible half-ready.

    db.users.createIndex(
  { email: 1 },
  { unique: true, hidden: true, name: "email_1_new" }
)
db.users.unhideIndex("email_1_new")

    Rebuild only for a concrete reason: changing options (unique, partial, collation), suspected corruption, or a badly fragmented index. Routine rebuilding is not part of MongoDB maintenance and costs a full collection scan plus heavy I/O.

    289. What does it mean when an index is "building"?

    It means the server is scanning the collection to populate the index entries, and until that finishes the index exists in the catalog but the query planner will not use it. Builds run in a hybrid mode: they take a brief exclusive lock on the collection to start and finish, but yield to reads and writes in between, so the collection stays available.

    You can watch progress with db.currentOp({ "msg": "Index Build" }), which reports the index name and a progress percentage, or through db.adminCommand({ currentOp: 1, $all: true }) on a replica set. A build that seems stuck is usually I/O bound or waiting for a long-running transaction to finish. Dropping the index while it builds aborts the build.

    290. How do you check how much space the indexes on a collection take?

    db.users.stats() reports indexSizes as a document mapping each index name to its size in bytes, plus totalIndexSize. db.users.totalIndexSize() returns just the total, which is handy in a script.

    db.users.stats().totalIndexSize   // bytes
db.users.stats().indexSizes
// { _id_: 81920, email_1: 65536, country_1_createdAt_-1: 204800 }

    For a single index on a large collection, the aggregation form with $collStats and storageStats avoids pulling the whole stats document. This matters because indexes live in memory as well as on disk: if total index size greatly exceeds the WiredTiger cache, every query risks disk reads, and an oversized unused index is a good candidate for dropping.

    291. How do you check the server version and the mongosh version?

    Inside the shell, db.version() returns the server version string such as 8.0.4; db.serverBuildInfo() adds the git commit, available storage engines and compile options, and db.hello() tells you whether you are connected to a primary or a secondary. The client version is not a shell method: run mongosh --version in your terminal, or check db.getMongo() in the shell for the connection object.

    db.version()          // "8.0.4"  (server)
mongosh --version     // "2.3.1"  (client, from the OS terminal)

    The distinction matters during upgrades and when a documented helper is missing: newer mongosh against an old server can behave differently, and features you read about may not exist on the server you reached.

    292. How do you filter db.currentOp() to find long-running operations?

    Pass a filter document instead of dumping every operation. The most useful fields are active, secs_running, ns and op.

    db.currentOp({
  active: true,
  secs_running: { $gt: 5 },
  ns: { $ne: "" }
})

    For each result read opid (needed for killing), op (query, update, command), ns, secs_running, planSummary (COLLSCAN on a huge collection is a red flag), numYields, waitingForLock and client. If nothing shows up, remember that idle connections and drivers' pooled sockets appear as inactive, and that the shell only sees operations on the node it is connected to.

    293. How do you kill a long-running operation with killOp?

    Find the operation id first, then kill it from the admin database. The id is an opid, not a session or thread id.

    db.currentOp({ active: true, secs_running: { $gt: 30 } })
db.adminCommand({ killOp: 1, op: 123456 })

    The server interrupts the operation at its next yield point, so a long index build or a large update may take a moment to stop, and the client receives an error such as operation was interrupted. Some internal operations cannot be killed safely, and killing a write mid-flight can leave partial work unless it was in a transaction. Always confirm with currentOp afterwards, and prefer fixing the query or index over killing operations routinely.

    294. In db.serverStatus(), what should you look at first?

    Start with three areas. Connections: compare connections.current with connections.available; being close to the limit means pool leaks or undersized limits. Queues and locks: non-zero globalLock.currentQueue.readers/writers shows contention. WiredTiger cache: check wiredTiger.cache["bytes currently in the cache"] against ["maximum bytes configured"] and watch the tracked dirty bytes, since a cache at capacity with high dirty pages turns reads into disk I/O.

    db.serverStatus().connections
db.serverStatus().globalLock.currentQueue
db.serverStatus().wiredTiger.cache["bytes currently in the cache"]

    Then glance at opcounters for traffic shape and asserts for errors. The document is huge, so query the specific path instead of printing all of it.

    295. How do you rename a collection, and how do you rename a field?

    Rename a collection with renameCollection, which needs the admin database and takes a lock; both names must be in the same database, and dropTarget: true overwrites an existing target.

    db.adminCommand({
  renameCollection: "shop.orders_old",
  to: "shop.orders",
  dropTarget: true
})

    Rename a field inside documents with the $rename update operator, using a dotted path for nested fields:

    db.users.updateMany({}, { $rename: { "name": "fullName" } })
db.users.updateMany({}, { $rename: { "address.zip": "address.postalCode" } })

    $rename is a per-document update, not an atomic schema change: it overwrites the target field if it exists, does not work on array elements, and on a large collection should be run in batches so you can throttle and resume.

    296. How do you use $slice in a find projection to return only part of an array?

    $slice in a projection trims an array after the document is matched, without changing which documents are returned. Three forms are worth memorising.

    db.posts.find({}, { comments: { $slice: 3 } })     // first 3
db.posts.find({}, { comments: { $slice: -3 } })    // last 3
db.posts.find({}, { comments: { $slice: [10, 5] } }) // skip 10, take 5

    It is the standard way to avoid shipping a 500-element array to a mobile client, and it can be combined with other projection fields. The catch: the filter runs first, so you cannot ask for documents whose sliced window contains a value; that needs $filter or an aggregation with $slice as an expression. Also remember the document on disk is still read in full.

    297. How do you create a read-only view and control who can read it?

    A view is a saved aggregation pipeline over a collection or another view. It stores no data, is computed on every read, and is inherently read-only: insert, update and delete are rejected against it.

    db.createView("publicUsers", "users", [
  { $match: { active: true } },
  { $project: { name: 1, city: 1, email: 0, ssn: 0 } }
])

    Grant privileges on the view's own namespace rather than the base collection, so a reporting user can read the projection but not the raw documents:

    db.createRole({
  role: "publicUsersReader",
  privileges: [{ resource: { db: "shop", collection: "publicUsers" },
                 actions: ["find"] }],
  roles: []
})
db.grantRolesToUser("reporting", [{ role: "publicUsersReader", db: "shop" }])

    Typical uses are hiding PII, freezing a stable field shape for consumers, and pre-filtering tenants.

    298. What is a namespace in MongoDB?

    A namespace is the fully qualified name of a collection or index in the form database.collection, for example shop.orders. It is the identifier the catalog, the query planner and every diagnostic surface use: you see it as the ns field in currentOp, in slow-query log lines, in profiler output, in lock reports and in error messages such as a duplicate key error naming shop.orders.

    Namespaces matter operationally because MongoDB documents a length limit (120 bytes for a collection namespace, 255 for index namespaces), so extremely long database or collection names can be rejected. Views, time series collections and GridFS buckets all occupy their own namespaces, which is why a dump of a "single" logical dataset often shows several entries.

    299. Why can't { field: { $exists: false } } use an index the way { field: null } can?

    Because the two predicates are not the same. { field: null } matches documents where the field is explicitly null or missing, and a normal (non-sparse) index stores missing fields as null entries, so the planner can seek to them. $exists: false matches only missing fields, and those documents are precisely the ones a sparse index omits, so a sparse index cannot answer it and the server falls back to a collection scan.

    db.users.find({ deletedAt: null })          // can use index on deletedAt
db.users.find({ deletedAt: { $exists: false } }) // usually COLLSCAN
db.users.find({ deletedAt: { $exists: true } })  // can use a sparse index

    That asymmetry is a good reason to write explicit nulls instead of omitting fields when you plan to query on absence.

    300. How should a UUID be stored: as BinData or as a string?

    Store it as BSON binary with the UUID subtype (subtype 4 for a standard RFC 4122 UUID), using the driver's native UUID type rather than a hand-made buffer.

    // mongosh
db.devices.insertOne({
  _id: UUID("3f2504e0-4f89-11d3-9a0c-0305e82c3301")
})

// Node.js driver
await devices.insertOne({ _id: new UUID("3f2504e0-4f89-11d3-9a0c-0305e82c3301") })

    Binary form is 16 bytes against roughly 36 for the canonical string, so indexes are smaller, comparisons follow byte order, and there are no case or collation surprises. The string form is friendlier for debugging, URLs and CSV round trips, but it doubles storage and invites the classic bug of mixing uppercase and lowercase representations of the same identifier. Whichever you choose, keep one format for the field across the whole system.

    Intermediate 380 questions

    Beyond the basics: index design and the query planner, replication and sharding in practice, aggregation at depth, schema patterns, change streams, transactions, backup, upgrades and driver behaviour.

    301. How does WiredTiger implement B-tree indexes, and what makes them different from a simple in-memory map?

    WiredTiger stores each index as a B-tree of pages, with internal pages holding separator keys and leaf pages holding the actual key and record-id pairs. Pages are cached uncompressed in memory and compressed on disk, and they are written as immutable blocks so readers never block writers. The B-tree is updated in place within the cache but persisted through checkpointing, which makes index lookups logarithmic in page count rather than linear scans. This structure is why index depth and page size matter for read latency.

    302. What does index selectivity mean and why is it more useful than raw cardinality?

    Cardinality is the number of distinct values in a field, while selectivity describes how well a predicate filters the collection. A field can have high cardinality but still be a poor index if most queries match a large fraction of documents. Good indexes typically match a small percentage of the collection, so the planner prefers them over collection scans. Measure real selectivity from explain output, not from the number of distinct values alone.

    303. Explain the ESR rule for designing compound indexes.

    ESR stands for Equality, Sort, Range: fields used in equality predicates should come first, then fields used for sorting, then fields used in range predicates. Following this order lets the index satisfy equality filters, deliver results already sorted, and still bound the range scan efficiently. Placing a range field before a sort field forces an in-memory sort because the index order is broken after the range. The rule is a guideline, and the planner's actual choice should be confirmed with explain.

    // Query: status = "active", sort by createdAt, amount > 100
db.orders.createIndex({ status: 1, createdAt: 1, amount: 1 })

    304. What is a multikey index and which operations does it not support well?

    A multikey index is created automatically when an indexed field holds an array, and MongoDB writes one index entry per array element. It accelerates queries that match array elements, but a single compound index cannot have more than one array field, because that would create a Cartesian product of entries. Multikey indexes also cannot provide a covered query when the array field itself is returned, since the full array is not stored in the index. Sorting on a multikey field can require a blocking sort because the same document appears under several keys.

    305. What is the index prefix rule and how does it affect compound index usability?

    A compound index can serve queries that use any contiguous prefix of its fields, starting from the first field. An index on {a: 1, b: 1, c: 1} supports queries on a, on a plus b, and on all three, but not on b alone. This is why field order in a compound index is a design decision, not a formality. An index on the reverse order is a separate index and is not covered by the prefix rule.

    306. What is a covered query and what conditions must hold for one?

    A covered query is answered entirely from the index, with no document fetch. Every field in the filter and in the projection must be present in the index, and the query must not return the full document. The plan shows IXSCAN with no FETCH stage. Index-only plans reduce I/O but can be slower than expected if the index entries are large.

    db.users.createIndex({ email: 1, name: 1 })
db.users.find({ email: "a@b.co" }, { _id: 0, email: 1, name: 1 })

    307. How do unique, sparse, and partial indexes differ?

    A unique index rejects duplicate values, but by default only one document may have the field missing, because missing counts as null. A sparse index skips documents where the field is absent, which removes that null collision but also makes the index unusable for queries that need those documents. A partial index uses a filter expression to index only matching documents, giving finer control plus a smaller index. Partial indexes are usually the better choice for selective uniqueness.

    db.users.createIndex(
  { email: 1 },
  { unique: true, partialFilterExpression: { email: { $exists: true } } }
)

    308. How do TTL indexes work and what are their limitations?

    A TTL index is a single-field index on a date or array-of-dates field with an expireAfterSeconds option. A background thread removes expired documents, typically within about a minute of expiry, so deletion is not exact. TTL indexes cannot be compound, and the field must be a date or an array of dates. Expiry is based on the indexed value, not on document insertion time, so clock skew in application writes matters.

    db.sessions.createIndex({ lastSeen: 1 }, { expireAfterSeconds: 3600 })

    309. How do text indexes work and what do field weights control?

    A text index tokenizes string fields, stems words, and stores term postings so that $text queries can search and rank matches. Weights set the relative importance of indexed fields in the computed text score, so a title match can outrank a body match. A collection may have at most one text index, though it can span many fields. Text indexes are case-insensitive and ignore stop words by default, and they do not support language-specific analysis beyond the specified default language.

    db.articles.createIndex(
  { title: "text", body: "text" },
  { weights: { title: 10, body: 1 } }
)

    310. When would you use a wildcard index, and what is the cost?

    A wildcard index indexes all fields, or all fields under a given path, which suits schemas with unpredictable or highly polymorphic field names. It removes the need to pre-declare an index per attribute, but it writes one index entry per field per document, so it grows faster and consumes more memory than a targeted index. Wildcard indexes do not support shard keys and have restrictions on compound use. They should be a fallback when the schema genuinely cannot be enumerated.

    db.products.createIndex({ "attributes.$**": 1 })

    311. What is a hidden index and why would you hide one?

    A hidden index is maintained by the storage engine but ignored by the query planner. It lets you test the impact of dropping an index without paying the cost of a rebuild if the removal turns out to be harmful. You can unhide it instantly to restore planner visibility. Hiding is useful during index audits when you want to confirm an index is genuinely unused.

    db.orders.hideIndex("status_1")
db.orders.unhideIndex("status_1")

    312. What is a rolling index build and when does it matter?

    A rolling build creates the index on one replica set member at a time, typically starting with a secondary, so the set never loses all redundant capacity at once. Modern MongoDB builds indexes concurrently on secondaries by default, so the classic background-build flag is no longer the mechanism to rely on. Rolling builds still matter for large collections where an index build could saturate I/O or stall replication. Plan them during low-traffic windows and watch replication lag between steps.

    313. How does index size affect memory and overall performance?

    WiredTiger keeps hot index pages in its cache, so a large index competes with document data for the same memory budget. When index pages are evicted, lookups require disk reads, which raises latency even if the plan is otherwise optimal. Wide compound indexes and multikey indexes on large arrays amplify this effect. Auditing rarely used indexes is as important as adding missing ones.

    314. Does MongoDB use index intersection, and how reliable is it?

    MongoDB can intersect results from multiple indexes for a single query, but it rarely does so and the planner usually prefers one well-chosen compound index. Intersection costs extra work per index and per matching document, so it is often slower than a single selective index. You should not design a schema expecting intersection to substitute for a compound index. If explain shows an intersection plan, consider whether a compound index would be simpler and faster.

    315. How does the query planner choose a plan, and what role does the plan cache play?

    The planner generates candidate plans, runs them in a trial period against a sample of the work, and keeps the plan that returns results with the least work. The chosen plan is cached by query shape, letting later executions skip the trial phase. The cache is invalidated by index changes, collection drops, and explicit clears. A cached plan can become stale when data distribution shifts, which is why plan cache inspection is part of performance work.

    316. What do the explain() verbosity levels show?

    queryPlanner shows the winning plan and rejected candidates without executing the query. executionStats runs the query and reports actual keys and documents examined, timing, and rows returned. allPlansExecution adds execution statistics for the rejected candidate plans, which is useful when you want to know why a plan lost. Use executionStats for routine tuning and allPlansExecution when the winning plan looks wrong.

    db.orders.find({ status: "paid" }).explain("executionStats")

    317. Which executionStats fields matter most when diagnosing a slow query?

    totalKeysExamined, totalDocsExamined, and nReturned together reveal how efficiently the plan filters. An ideal selective query has these three numbers close to each other, while a large gap between keys examined and documents returned suggests a weak index or a filter applied after the scan. Also check executionTimeMillis and whether the plan contains a blocking SORT. Comparing ratios is more informative than looking at absolute timings alone.

    318. What is the difference between COLLSCAN, IXSCAN, and FETCH in a query plan?

    COLLSCAN reads every document in the collection and evaluates the filter, which is linear in collection size. IXSCAN walks an index to find matching keys, then FETCH retrieves the full documents for those keys. A plan with IXSCAN and no FETCH is covered. Seeing COLLSCAN on a hot query is the clearest signal that an index is missing or unusable.

    319. What causes a blocking SORT stage and how do you remove it?

    A blocking sort appears when no index can supply the requested order, so the server must buffer and sort documents in memory. It is especially costly when the sort exceeds the memory limit, which produces an error rather than a silent spill. To remove it, create an index whose key order matches the sort and whose leading fields satisfy the query's equality predicates. Verify success by confirming the plan has no SORT stage and hasSortStage is false.

    320. How do you find and clear rejected plans in the plan cache, and why would you?

    You can list cached plans with $planCache.list and inspect their scores and whether they are marked inactive. Clearing a single shape with $planCache.clearPlansByQuery or the whole cache with $planCache.clear forces replanning. This helps when a plan was cached under an unrepresentative data distribution, for example right after a bulk load. Clearing the whole cache is a blunt instrument that briefly increases planning cost.

    db.orders.getPlanCache().clear()
db.orders.getPlanCache().list()

    321. How and when should you use hint()?

    hint() forces the planner to use a named index or key pattern, bypassing plan selection. It is appropriate when you have proven that the planner consistently picks a worse index, or when you need a predictable plan for a latency-sensitive path. Hints are brittle: dropping or renaming the index breaks the query, and a hint can become suboptimal as data changes. Reassess hints after major data or index changes instead of leaving them indefinitely.

    db.orders.find({ status: "paid" }).hint({ status: 1, createdAt: -1 })

    322. How do you use $indexStats to find unused indexes?

    $indexStats reports per-index access counters, including the number of operations that used each index since the last restart or index creation. Indexes with zero accesses over a representative period, including a full business cycle, are candidates for removal. The counters reset on restart, so a short observation window can be misleading. Combine the stats with knowledge of ad-hoc or rare queries before dropping anything.

    db.orders.aggregate([{ $indexStats: {} }])

    323. Describe the architecture of a replica set and the role of each member type.

    A replica set has one primary that accepts writes and any number of secondaries that replicate the oplog and can serve reads. An arbiter participates in elections but holds no data, so it can tip a vote without adding storage or read capacity. Deployments usually use three voting data-bearing members to tolerate one failure. Clients discover topology through the driver, which routes writes to the primary and reads according to the read preference.

    324. What is the oplog and how does replication actually apply writes?

    The oplog is a capped collection on every member that records idempotent write operations in a total order. Secondaries tail the primary's oplog, fetch the referenced documents when needed, and apply the operations to their own data. Because entries are idempotent, a secondary can re-apply an operation safely after a restart. The oplog is the mechanism for both replication and change stream history.

    325. What causes replication lag and how do you monitor it?

    Lag grows when a secondary cannot apply oplog entries as fast as the primary produces them, often due to slow disk, insufficient CPU, long-running index builds, or network saturation. rs.printSecondaryReplicationInfo() and the replication lag fields in serverStatus show the gap in seconds. Persistent lag risks the secondary falling off the oplog, which requires a full resync. Alert on lag trends rather than single spikes, since brief spikes are normal under bursty writes.

    rs.printSecondaryReplicationInfo()

    326. Explain write concern levels and the role of journaling.

    Write concern describes how many members must acknowledge a write before the client considers it successful. w: 1 acknowledges on the primary's memory, w: "majority" waits for most voting data-bearing members, and j: true additionally requires the write to be journaled. Higher acknowledgement reduces the chance of rollback on failover at the cost of latency. Durable critical writes should combine majority acknowledgement with journaling.

    db.orders.insertOne(doc, { writeConcern: { w: "majority", j: true } })

    327. Compare read concern levels local, majority, and snapshot.

    local returns data as the queried member currently has it, with no guarantee it will not be rolled back. majority returns data that has been acknowledged by a majority and is therefore durable against failover. snapshot provides a consistent point-in-time view, and outside transactions it is tied to majority-committed data. Use snapshot inside multi-document transactions when you need a stable view across reads.

    328. What are the read preference modes and when do you use each?

    primary sends all reads to the primary, guaranteeing the freshest data. primaryPreferred falls back to a secondary only when no primary is available. secondary sends reads to secondaries, and secondaryPreferred falls back to the primary when no secondary is reachable. nearest picks the lowest-latency eligible member, which may be the primary. Secondary reads can return stale data, so they suit analytics and reporting rather than read-your-writes flows.

    329. How does causal consistency work with afterClusterTime?

    A session tracks an operation time as it performs reads and writes, and subsequent operations in the same session send that time as afterClusterTime. The server then waits until the member has applied at least that oplog timestamp before answering. This guarantees read-your-writes and monotonic reads across members without requiring primary reads. Causal consistency requires majority read concern and is available in sessions on replica sets and sharded clusters.

    const session = db.getMongo().startSession();
session.startTransaction({ readConcern: { level: "majority" } });

    330. How do elections work and what determines which member becomes primary?

    Members hold elections when they detect that the primary is unreachable, and a candidate needs votes from a majority of voting members to win. Higher priority members are more likely to be elected, and equal priorities are broken by the most recent oplog entry. Elections take seconds but cause a brief window with no primary. Setting priorities deliberately keeps preferred hardware in the primary role.

    331. What happens during a stepdown and how do drivers handle failover?

    A stepdown makes the primary relinquish its role, usually triggered by replSetStepDown or by another member with higher priority becoming eligible. In-progress writes can fail with a retryable error while a new primary is elected. Modern drivers automatically retry retryable writes and certain reads once, hiding most of the disruption. Applications should still handle the case where a retry is refused because the operation is not idempotent by design.

    rs.stepDown(60)

    332. What are hidden and delayed replica set members used for?

    A hidden member replicates data but is invisible to client read preferences, so it takes no application traffic. A delayed member applies oplog entries after a fixed delay, providing a window to recover from accidental deletes or bad deployments. Both must have priority zero so they never become primary. Delayed members are a safety net, not a backup replacement.

    333. What is the majority commit point and why does it matter?

    The majority commit point is the most recent oplog timestamp that a majority of data-bearing members have acknowledged. Reads at majority read concern and w: "majority" writes are defined relative to this point. Data beyond it can be rolled back if the primary fails before replication completes. Monitoring the commit point relative to the primary's latest entry reveals replication health.

    334. Describe the components of a sharded cluster.

    A sharded cluster consists of shards that hold subsets of the data, mongos routers that direct client operations, and config servers that store cluster metadata such as chunk ranges and shard membership. Applications connect to mongos and are unaware of which shard holds a document. The config servers form their own replica set, and each shard is itself a replica set. Metadata consistency across config servers is essential for correct routing.

    335. What is a chunk and how does it relate to sharding?

    A chunk is a contiguous range of shard key values assigned to a shard, and it is the unit of data movement during balancing. MongoDB splits chunks that exceed the configured maximum size and migrates them to even out distribution. Chunk count is metadata, not physical storage granularity, so many small chunks increase metadata overhead. The balancer moves chunks, not individual documents.

    336. How do you choose a good shard key?

    A good shard key has high cardinality, low frequency for common values, and a distribution that grows without hotspots. It should also appear in most queries so that operations can be targeted rather than broadcast. Monotonically increasing keys such as timestamps concentrate writes on the last chunk, creating a hot shard. Shard keys are difficult to change, so the decision deserves up-front modelling of query and write patterns.

    337. Compare hashed and ranged shard keys.

    A hashed shard key distributes documents pseudo-randomly, which spreads writes evenly and avoids monotonic hotspots, but it cannot serve range queries efficiently because ordering is lost. A ranged shard key preserves order, enabling targeted range scans and better locality, at the risk of uneven distribution. Hashed keys are a good default for high-volume insert workloads with point lookups. Choose ranged keys when range queries on the key are common and you can design a compound prefix to avoid hotspots.

    338. What is the difference between a targeted query and a scatter-gather query?

    A targeted query includes the shard key, so mongos can route it to a single shard or a small set of shards. A scatter-gather query omits the shard key and must be broadcast to every shard, then merged, which scales with shard count. Scatter-gather is acceptable for rare admin queries but damaging on hot paths as the cluster grows. Query shapes, not just index design, determine sharding performance.

    339. How does the balancer work and when can it hurt performance?

    The balancer runs on the primary config server and migrates chunks between shards when distribution crosses configured thresholds. Migrations consume network and disk I/O on both source and target shards, which can affect latency. You can schedule balancing windows or temporarily stop the balancer during bulk loads. A cluster with a poorly chosen shard key may balance constantly without ever achieving even distribution.

    sh.stopBalancer()
sh.startBalancer()

    340. What are sharding zones and what problems do they solve?

    Zones associate ranges of shard key values with specific shards, letting you pin data to geographic regions or to dedicated hardware tiers. They support data residency requirements and workload isolation, for example keeping EU customer data on EU shards. Each shard can belong to multiple zones and each zone can cover multiple ranges. Every range covered by a zone must have at least one shard in that zone, otherwise writes fail.

    341. How do change streams work and what is a resume token?

    Change streams expose a real-time, resumable cursor over oplog-derived events for a collection, database, or deployment. Each event carries a resume token, and passing the last token in resumeAfter lets a consumer continue exactly where it stopped after a disconnect. Tokens remain valid as long as the corresponding oplog history exists, so consumers must persist them durably. Change streams require a replica set or sharded cluster and a read concern that supports majority-committed history.

    const cs = db.orders.watch([{ $match: { "operationType": "insert" } }]);

    342. How do multi-document transactions work with sessions and retries?

    Transactions are scoped to a session, started with startTransaction, and committed or aborted explicitly. They provide snapshot isolation, and conflicting writes cause a TransientTransactionError that should be retried as a whole. Commit itself can fail with an unknown result, which requires retrying commitTransaction with the same transaction number. Transactions have a default lifetime limit and should be short and limited in scope.

    343. What is the bucket schema pattern and when does it help?

    The bucket pattern groups many small related documents into a single document, often with a count and a bounded array, reducing document count and index size. It suits high-volume time series or event data where reads fetch a window rather than a single item. Buckets must be bounded so they do not approach the 16 MB document limit. Time series collections apply a similar internal bucketing strategy automatically.

    { sensorId: "s1", start: ISODate("2026-01-01"), count: 3,
  readings: [ { t: 1, v: 20 }, { t: 2, v: 21 }, { t: 3, v: 22 } ] }

    344. What is the computed pattern and what problem does it solve?

    The computed pattern precalculates expensive values at write time and stores them on the document, so reads avoid aggregation or joins. Typical examples are running totals, average ratings, or denormalized counts. The trade-off is that every write path must maintain the computed value consistently, which makes atomic updates and transactions important. Use it when reads vastly outnumber writes and the computation is expensive.

    345. When would you use the extended reference pattern?

    The extended reference pattern embeds a small, frequently accessed subset of a referenced document into the referring document to avoid a lookup. For example, an order might store the customer's name alongside the customer id. It reduces read latency at the cost of duplicating data that may drift when the source changes. Keep the duplicated fields few, stable, and non-authoritative.

    346. What is the subset pattern and how does it control document growth?

    The subset pattern keeps a bounded, most-relevant portion of a large array in the main document and moves the rest to another collection. A product might embed its fifty newest reviews while older reviews live in a separate collection. This keeps documents small and hot reads fast without losing data. The application must manage the promotion and archival logic explicitly.

    347. What is the attribute pattern and when is it appropriate?

    The attribute pattern converts a set of sparse, unpredictable fields into an array of key-value subdocuments, often paired with a wildcard or compound index on the key and value. It suits product catalogs where each category has different attributes. Queries then target array elements rather than many optional top-level fields. The cost is slightly more complex query syntax and larger index entries.

    { sku: "A1", attrs: [ { k: "color", v: "red" }, { k: "size", v: "M" } ] }

    348. What is the polymorphic pattern and what does it cost?

    The polymorphic pattern stores documents of different shapes in one collection, using a shared discriminator field such as type to distinguish them. It keeps related entities together and avoids a proliferation of collections, which simplifies cross-type queries. The cost is that application code and indexes must handle divergent fields, and validation must be written per type. It works best when the variants share most of their structure.

    349. How do you decide between one-to-few, one-to-many, and one-to-squillions modelling?

    For one-to-few, embed the children because the array stays small and reads become a single document fetch. For one-to-many with a bounded but larger count, embed identifiers or use the subset pattern and reference the rest. For one-to-squillions, always reference from the child side, because an unbounded array risks the 16 MB limit and unbounded index growth. The decisive question is whether the array is bounded and whether it is read together with the parent.

    350. What are the real trade-offs between embedding and referencing?

    Embedding gives atomic single-document reads and writes, fewer round trips, and no application-level join, but it duplicates data and can grow without bound. Referencing keeps data normalized and avoids duplication, but requires $lookup or extra queries and cannot update related data atomically in one write. Access patterns should drive the decision: model for the queries you actually run, not for abstract purity. Hybrid approaches with small extended references are common in production schemas.

    351. When is denormalization justified for read performance?

    Denormalization is justified when a read path is hot, the duplicated data changes rarely, and the cost of a join or second query dominates latency. It trades write complexity and storage for read speed, so the write paths must be audited to keep copies consistent. Document the authoritative source of each duplicated field to avoid divergent updates. If the duplicated value changes often, denormalization usually causes more bugs than it saves milliseconds.

    352. How do you handle schema versioning and migrations in MongoDB?

    Add a schemaVersion field to documents and let the application read multiple versions, upgrading lazily on write or through a background job. This avoids a stop-the-world migration and keeps rolling deployments safe. For bulk changes, use batched updates with a filter on the old version and monitor progress. Keep both readers and writers compatible during the transition window.

    db.users.updateMany(
  { schemaVersion: 1 },
  { $set: { schemaVersion: 2, "prefs.theme": "light" } }
)

    353. What are time series collections and what do they optimize?

    Time series collections store measurements in internal buckets grouped by series and time window, which compresses data and reduces index overhead. They provide specialized indexes and support window functions and downsampling through aggregation. Writes go through a bucketing layer, so updating or deleting individual measurements is more restricted than in a normal collection. They are the recommended choice for metrics, sensor data, and event streams with a time dimension.

    db.createCollection("metrics", {
  timeseries: { timeField: "ts", metaField: "sensor", granularity: "minutes" }
})

    354. What does Atlas Search provide and how is it different from a text index?

    Atlas Search is a full-text search engine built on Apache Lucene, exposed through the $search aggregation stage. It supports analyzers, fuzzy matching, autocomplete, phrase and compound queries, and relevance scoring that the native text index cannot match. It maintains its own search index separate from MongoDB indexes, defined by a JSON index specification. Native text indexes remain simpler for basic keyword matching inside a self-managed deployment.

    db.products.aggregate([
  { $search: { text: { query: "wireless", path: "name" } } }
])

    355. What is Atlas Vector Search and what is it used for?

    Atlas Vector Search indexes high-dimensional embeddings and supports approximate nearest-neighbour queries through the $vectorSearch stage. It enables semantic search and retrieval-augmented generation by comparing query embeddings with stored document embeddings. You must generate embeddings outside the database and store them in an array field with a matching index definition. Results are ranked by vector similarity and can be combined with filters or a search stage.

    { $vectorSearch: { index: "vec", path: "embedding",
  queryVector: q, numCandidates: 100, limit: 10 } }

    356. How does connection pooling work and how do you size maxPoolSize?

    Drivers maintain a pool of connections per server in the topology and check out a connection per operation, returning it when the operation completes. maxPoolSize caps concurrent connections, and a value too low causes checkout queueing under load while too high exhausts server file descriptors and memory. Size it from concurrent request volume and average operation duration, then validate with server-side connection counts. In serverless or autoscaling environments, keep pools small per instance and account for total instance count.

    mongodb://host/?maxPoolSize=50&minPoolSize=5

    357. What is a write conflict in WiredTiger and how is it resolved?

    WiredTiger uses optimistic concurrency: a write conflict occurs when two operations try to modify the same document concurrently. One operation succeeds and the other receives a WriteConflict error, which is a transient error that the server or driver retries. Long-running transactions and hot documents increase conflict rates. Reducing transaction scope and avoiding read-modify-write patterns on the same document lowers contention.

    358. Why can concurrent upserts create duplicate documents and how do you prevent it?

    An upsert is a read followed by a conditional insert, so two concurrent upserts with no matching document can both attempt an insert. A unique index on the filter fields makes one of them fail with a duplicate key error, which the application should treat as a signal to retry as an update. Without a unique index there is no server-side protection. Always pair upsert logic with a unique constraint on the identity fields.

    db.users.createIndex({ email: 1 }, { unique: true })
db.users.updateOne({ email: e }, { $setOnInsert: { createdAt: new Date() } }, { upsert: true })
